##### **Robust Counterfactuals for Markov Decision Processes under Statistical and Causal Uncertainties**
2610.20210v1 by Jessica Lally, Milad Kazemi, Nicola Paoletti

Counterfactual reasoning (asking what would have happened, had a decision been different) is a powerful tool for identifying causes of failure and explaining the behaviour of AI systems in sequential decision-making tasks. In Markov Decision Processes (MDPs), two sources of uncertainty complicate counterfactual inference: statistical uncertainty in the MDP transitions when the MDP is estimated from trajectory data, and uncertainty about the unknown causal model, as there are multiple structural causal models (SCMs) compatible with each MDP. Existing work addresses these two challenges in isolation. We address both simultaneously by introducing a framework for partial counterfactual inference in MDPs that is robust to both statistical and causal uncertainty. Our approach provides: 1) closed-form analytical bounds for the counterfactual probabilities that are valid across all SCMs entailed by a given interval MDP (IMDP); 2) a method to compute minimal robust interventions to achieve a desired counterfactual outcome via pessimistic value iteration on the induced counterfactual IMDP. Since the latter is constructed using our analytical bounds, our approach remains efficient and scalable to realistic MDP environments. Experiments across multiple case studies demonstrate our method's robustness and practical utility for identifying minimal sets of actions that, if changed, would have led to improved outcomes.

To probe whether query time alone determines this response, we fix the midpoint query and current/subsequent guidance while changing only earlier CFG. In held-out factorial outcomes on five previously studied checkpoints, the mean midpoint horizon benefit increases from 0.109 to 1.308 FID, indicating sensitivity to the incoming-state distribution. A frozen four-query rule reduces mean CIFAR-10 FID by 2.023 without retraining or extra queries, including gains on five checkpoints trained after rule selection. A milder rule also improves mean FID by 1.041 on a stronger frozen grid, with fixed-rule FID gains on the evaluated public ImageNet-256 and AFHQ checkpoints. However, matched-budget calibration favors query-start shifting in historical CIFAR tests, and the tested Horizon allocation adds no FID benefit beyond Grid+Start on two public ImageNet models. Thus, prediction horizon is a distinct, position- and state-sensitive inference control, but not a universally preferred one.

摘要：

##### **Trainable at Depth, Solvable at Small Size: What the Loss Surface of a Polynomial Activation Dictates for Designing, Initializing and Training Networks**
2610.20069v1 by Xiuyi Yang

What makes a deep network train, and what shape is its loss surface? We answer both with one construction at two scales: a polynomial activation f (z) = z + λz 3 , clamped and projected onto the unit sphere, plus an unnormalized identity path. As a deep stack it trains to one hundred layers on MNIST with no normalization machinery; as a small model its square loss is a polynomial system we solve exactly. The small scale explains the large one: it prescribes how to design, initialize and train the stack.

摘要：

##### **Which Knowledge Should LLMs Trust? Uncovering a Generalizable Steering Direction Across Knowledge Conflicts**
2610.20067v1 by Yibin Lou, Yi Hu, Jianhui Chen, Chenpeng Wang, Muhan Zhang, Liangming Pan

Large Language Models (LLMs) frequently encounter conflicts between internal parametric knowledge and external contextual knowledge, yet the mechanisms governing this selection remain unclear. We show that the choice between context and parametric knowledge is linearly represented and can be controlled along a single direction in the model's residual stream. We first demonstrate that this direction generalizes across factual-conflict settings, including context formats, knowledge categories, and datasets, while consistently shifting behavior between parametric and contextual answers. We then transfer a direction learned from simple factual conflicts to three more complex task families: RAG, memory, and rule following, where models must acquire information from retrieved documents, interaction histories, or explicit rules. Across four model families and multiple transfer settings, steering consistently improves the use of prompt-provided information over the unsteered model. These results suggest that the direction captures a transferable preference for external knowledge rather than a mechanism specific to factual conflicts.

摘要：

##### **FoveaVLA: Foveation Actions Replace the Wrist Camera in VLAs**
2610.20064v1 by Bingao Chen, Lihan Zha, C. Karen Liu, Guy Tevet

We present FoveaVLA, a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model that replaces the wrist camera with learned foveation actions. Wrist cameras provide close-up detail but restrict the view to the end-effector's surroundings. External cameras see the whole scene, but the policy receives them at low resolution, so the fine detail needed for manipulation is lost. FoveaVLA recovers this detail by predicting, alongside each action chunk, the interaction region to observe at the next policy query. The policy then receives crops of these regions, taken from the original-resolution images, together with the full views. We build FoveaVLA on $π_{0.5}$ in two configurations, Single and Dual, using one or two external stereo cameras with full and foveal RGB and depth inputs, and post-train both on DROID with RoboInter interaction annotations. Across four real-world task suites, FoveaVLA-Single achieves 70.8% success with one external RGB-D view, and FoveaVLA-Dual achieves 77.1%, versus 52.1% for the wrist-equipped $π_{0.5}$. Both match the baseline on plain pick-and-place and improve success with distractors, cloth manipulation, and far-away objects.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Correctness: Compute-Responsive Data Selection for Reasoning Model Post-Training**
2610.20057v1 by Liang He, Xiangyu Li, Yuxin Wang, Hanbin Zhang, Mingyu Cao, Zhaomiao Guo, Xilu Wang

Reasoning language models increasingly rely on test-time compute scaling, yet most existing work studies how to allocate inference computation while treating the model itself as fixed. We ask a complementary question: can reasoning models be post-trained to make better use of a given amount of test-time compute, and which examples are most useful for this goal? Our key observation is that reasoning problems differ substantially in their response to additional computation. Some are already solved under limited compute, others remain unresolved even after further reasoning, while a distinct subset changes from complete initial failure to success when additional reasoning is provided. We call these compute-responsive (CR) examples and introduce Compute-Responsive Data Selection (CRDS), a post-training data-selection principle that prioritizes examples with empirically demonstrated marginal utility from additional reasoning. On Qwen3-8B, SFT on CR examples recovers 45.3\% of held-out low-compute failures, compared with 40.6\% when training on already-solved examples. The advantage extends beyond the selected CR split: SFT-CR reaches 51.8\% overall accuracy on an independently sampled test distribution, with the gain concentrated on compute-responsive problems. CR training also shifts the compute--accuracy frontier across inference budgets from 512 to 4,096 tokens, outperforming matched non-CR training throughout and reaching 48.1\% recovery at the largest budget. The same pattern is supported across multiple data sources, remains visible under on-policy distillation, and extends to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct when CR sets are reconstructed separately for that model. Better test-time compute utilization can therefore be learned through post-training, with recoverable responses to additional reasoning providing a particularly informative signal for selecting training examples.

摘要：

##### **GeoCloak: An Agentic Framework for Semantics-Preserving Geolocation Privacy via Peripheral Clue Manipulation**
2610.20055v1 by Yixin Wu, Mingjie Li, Yun Shen, Michael Backes, Yang Zhang

The rapid advancement of large vision language models (LVLMs) has made geolocation inference from images highly accurate and accessible. This poses serious privacy risks: a passive adversary with black-box LVLM access can infer a user's location from a single photo with no metadata. Existing protection methods rely almost exclusively on adversarial perturbation, which introduces perceptible visual artifacts and utility degradation. We identify the visual-geolocation asymmetry: geolocation-decisive clues, such as street signs, tend to be visually peripheral but dominate location inference. Exploiting this asymmetry, we propose GeoCloak, an agentic framework for semantics-preserving geolocation privacy protection. It acts as a clue-aware planner that analyzes each image to identify geolocation-relevant clues, dispatches targeted semantic edits to directly suppress peripheral clues, and autonomously configures adversarial perturbation to further suppress residual geolocation features, all under iterative utility-aware feedback. We evaluate GeoCloak on four diverse datasets spanning 108 countries and 576 cities against five LVLMs and three inference methods. GeoCloak reduces overall city-level accuracy from 41.2% to 9.9%, approximately half of the best-performing baseline, while achieving substantially superior utility preservation both qualitatively and quantitatively. We hope this work takes a meaningful step toward practical, semantics-preserving geolocation privacy protection, giving users greater control over the location information inadvertently embedded in their shared images.

摘要：

##### **What Is a Clarification? Estimating Model-Dependent Input Ambiguity**
2610.20052v1 by Jingqin Tang, Tim Tomov, Sebastian Schmidt, Dominik Fuchsgruber, Stephan Günnemann

Ambiguous inputs can admit multiple interpretations, each potentially yielding a different answer. Existing work largely treats this ambiguity as a property of the input, yet for a particular language model, only some alternatives may be known and answerable. We therefore distinguish world ambiguity, capturing all alternatives admitted by an input, from model ambiguity, capturing those accessible to the responding model. To characterize these alternatives, we formalize natural-language clarifications through three desiderata: \emph{uniqueness}, \emph{faithfulness}, and \emph{answer equivalence}. We show that these desiderata induce a one-to-one correspondence between valid answers and equivalence classes of clarifications, implying that any interpretation can be represented either by its answer or by the clarifications that uniquely express it. We leverage this correspondence to introduce \circleclar{}, a black-box method that estimates the set of answers available to a model by checking consistency along an answer--clarification--answer cycle. Across open-domain QA and executable Text-to-SQL, \circleclar{} achieves strong recovery of these answer sets. In QA, the recovered support yields the strongest overall performance in model-dependent ambiguity detection and enables effective selection among answering, clarifying, and abstaining. Together, these results demonstrate the practical value of a model-dependent view of ambiguity and its characterization through an answer-clarification correspondence.

摘要：

##### **SWE-Nexus: Automated Composition of Software Engineering Tasks from Test Execution**
2610.20038v1 by Songwei Yu, Ziqi Miao, Jingxuan Xu, Junhao Fan, Haoyang Huang, Yuan Xiong, Zihang Xu, Weihao Li, Wenqiang Zhu, Yuechen Luo, Xinshun Feng, Yanan Wu, Ken Deng

Historical code changes provide convenient task boundaries, but do not identify which production functions selected tests exercise or support composing validated behaviors beyond a single change. We present SWE-Nexus, a fully automated pipeline that constructs repository-level implementation tasks from test execution. Four construction agents select tests, adapt tracing and evaluation protocols to each repository, and generate behavioral specifications. For each selected test file, tracing records an observed execution footprint of production function bodies. A scope is retained only if all expected scoring points pass in the original repository and masking the traced bodies causes at least one failure or error. Compatible scopes are composed deterministically, with shared functions counted once and every constituent test file revalidated under the complete combined mask. The benchmark contains 600 tasks from 105 repositories across six programming languages, including 116 atomic and 484 composite tasks. We evaluate 12 models under three agent scaffolds. The best configuration resolves 37.8% of tasks in one attempt, and scope-weighted scoring measures partial recovery. SWE-Nexus enables task construction from validated behavior beyond individual historical changes.

摘要：

##### **LintEval: Benchmarking LLMs on Linter-Guided File Refactoring**
2610.20027v1 by Tongwei Deng, Jinyu Zhu, Yue Cao, Chenxu Wang, Yanzhen Zou, Bing Xie

Linters are widely used in code maintenance and real-time IDE editing to surface lint alerts related to performance, style, error-prone, and other patterns in source files. However, existing code benchmarks do not explicitly evaluate linter-guided file refactoring, where models must reduce lint alerts in source files with correct initial functionality while satisfying functionality preservation. We introduce LintEval, a multilingual benchmark for linter-guided file refactoring across C++, Python, and Java, comprising 13,061 programs with correct initial functionality and 171,662 lint alerts extracted using language-specific linters. LintEval jointly evaluates functionality preservation, lint alert reduction, new alert avoidance, and edit minimality. Experiments reveal a persistent trade-off: closed-source commercial models reach up to 90.4% Reduction@P but achieve only 67.1%-68.3% Pass@1, while several open-source small models show low or negative Reduction@P. A controlled fine-tuning study further shows that supervision format matters: refactoring-oriented SFT raises Qwen2.5-Coder-7B Reduction@P from 24.7% to 76.0%. LintEval enables controlled evaluation of models that improve source files under functionality preservation.

摘要：

##### **MorphAct: Robot Policies Morphing with the Worlds They Act In**
2610.20019v1 by Ziyan Liu, Jiting Liu, Weihong Zhong, Wenda Yu, Yeqiu Chen, Jason Li, Xiaojiang Peng, Shuyuan Hu, Kai Wang, Bo Zhao

Progress toward general intelligence in the physical world requires scaling robot learning across diverse environments and interactions. However, existing robot policies use shared parameters that remain fixed across interaction contexts. When different operations favor competing parameter adjustments, fitting their mixture can leave individual operations insufficiently adapted. Our atomic-task diagnostics reveal competing update directions in both vision-language and action parameters, with source-improving updates increasing loss on other tasks. This motivates MorphAct, a contextual parameterization framework that dynamically adjusts robot policy parameters as interaction contexts change. Two coupled generators produce context-dependent low-rank updates for the vision-language and action modules. Their coupling follows the policy's computational dependency: vision-language updates shape the representations that condition action-side parameter generation. Both generators are trained jointly through the native action objective, without operation labels or expert-adapter targets. MorphAct improves performance across three VLA backbones on LIBERO and Meta-World, with gains of up to 16.30%. In real-world evaluation, it achieves gains of up to 14% over $π_{0.5}$. Ablations support the benefits of contextual parameter generation and representation coupling.

摘要：

##### **What Coding Agents Leave Unsaid: Measuring Task-State Gaps Between Execution and Reports**
2610.20003v1 by Guoyang Xu, Rui Zhang, Xiuyu Wu, Hao Chen

Human supervisors rely on coding agents' reports to assess progress, verify repairs, and decide when to intervene. But a supervisor cannot investigate a failure they were never told occurred. Failed attempts, workarounds, and unresolved risks may disappear from routine reports, leaving supervisors unaware of issues that warrant scrutiny. We conduct CTXGAP, a measurement study quantifying the gap in task-state recoverability between agents' accumulated user-facing reports and their full recorded execution contexts. Across 37 coding tasks and 4,799 valid probes, agents answer 93.6% of task-state questions on demand; full-context readers recover 92.7%, while report-only readers recover 52.0%, a 40.7-point gap. At the final checkpoints of 30 verified fixes, the gap remains 34.8 points. Human annotation identifies states mentioned without enough supporting detail as the largest category of unrecovered information, and model-assessed supervision-relevant states retain substantial gaps. Full terminal scrollback improves recovery to 73.6% but still trails full context. Under matched report lengths, context-informed rewrites improve recovery by about eight points, without a detectable targeted-over-natural advantage. Positive gaps persist across harnesses and independent DeepSeek execution and measurement. CTXGAP establishes report-based task-state recoverability as a complementary evaluation dimension.

摘要：

##### **A Strong Harness Narrows the Model Gap: A Comparative Evaluation for Legislative Document-to-XML Conversion**
2610.19999v1 by Palvika Bansal, Rittika Jindal, Ruichong Wang

Legal-content reproduction requires matching the source exactly in section hierarchy, verbatim text, citations, and amendment spans; a dropped citation or mislabeled amendment carries into the products lawyers rely on. We report an industrial pipeline converting U.S. state legislation into strict, schema-constrained XML, and hypothesize that its accuracy comes from harness engineering, the deterministic tooling built around a model, more than the model itself. Our controlled ablation crosses three harnesses (zero-shot, a single tool-using agent, and a decomposed multi-agent pipeline) with three Claude models on 153 gold bills from two jurisdictions. The harness narrows the model gap: the weakest model in the full pipeline beats the strongest model alone (0.948 versus 0.806, paired bootstrap p less than 0.001). Because the two agentic harnesses share identical tools, their gap comes from orchestration: decomposition plus the persisted state and per-agent context it brings. Across four scored dimensions, the model spread at zero-shot (0.68 to 0.81) narrows to 0.95 at the full pipeline; the residual sits almost entirely in citation normalization, where the canonical identifier is retrieved from an external authority rather than generated from the weights, so a deterministic lookup tool supplies the exactness that a larger model does not.

摘要：

##### **Empirical Boundaries of Reservoir Computing for Few-Shot Sequence Pattern Tasks**
2610.19997v1 by Mario Mallea, Angela Nebot, Franscisco Mugica

Efficient sequence modeling remains a fundamental challenge in machine learning. Despite the success of Transformers, recurrent neural networks (RNNs) have recently re-emerged as efficient alternatives. Particularly, Echo State Networks (ESNs) offer a compelling framework of randomized RNNs that have been extensively studied for time-series forecasting and dynamical systems. This paper presents an empirical analysis of distributional, architectural, and optimization variants of ESNs evaluated on few-shot sequence-to-sequence (seq2seq) pattern tasks. These synthetic tasks utilize discrete symbol sequences, characterized by delays, inactive phases, and triggered markers to isolate and evaluate memory and pattern recognition capabilities. The minimality of these tasks is crucial to understand the strengths and limitations of machine learning models. Furthermore, when the input signal utilizes a one-hot encoding, ESN dynamics are governed strictly by their weight distributions, offering an ideal lens through which to study reservoir computing.
  Our findings map the performance boundaries of ESNs relative to state-of-the-art (SOTA) models. We show that preserving a linear state regime is vital for memory tasks, such as pattern completion or simple copy. We propose a data-dependent modulation mechanism for nonlinear input injection into the reservoir (G-ESN). G-ESN functions as a statistical filter capable of solving tasks that conventional ESNs cannot (the adding problem and bracket matching). Finally, for Deep ESNs, we evaluate a shared readout mechanism across layers that outperforms conventional concatenated readouts on structural tasks, including selective copy, sorting problem, and bracket matching.

摘要：

##### **Gray-Box Hallucination Detection in LLMs with Token Identities**
2610.19996v1 by Shaked Goldshtein, Yitzchak Grunbaum, Fabrizio Frasca, Guy Bar-Shalom, Haggai Maron

Large language models often produce fluent but factually incorrect output. Gray-box detectors identify such hallucinations from the token-probability statistics emitted during generation, avoiding the need for white-box access to hidden states that frontier models do not provide. These detectors, however, typically rely only on probability-derived representations and ignore the identities of the candidate tokens. They can therefore observe how uncertain the model is, but not what it is uncertain about. Consequently, they cannot distinguish between a model that is torn between "big" and "small" and one that is torn between "big" and "huge" when the two pairs receive the same probability distribution.
  We introduce GURI (Gray-box Uncertainty Representations with Identity), a lightweight gray-box hallucination detector that combines candidate-token probabilities with per-token vector identifiers. This exposes which alternatives the model considered while preserving the accessibility and transferability of the gray-box setting. Across three open-weight models and three question-answering benchmarks, GURI increases mean test ROC-AUC from 72.09% to 79.09% over a probability-only baseline. On the closed-source GPT-4.1 model, mean ROC-AUC increases from 67.39% to 71.84%. Among the three open-weight models, GURI outperforms transferred probability-only baseline in 17 of 18 directed cross-model transfers without fine-tuning. For the three open-weight models, cross-dataset transfer between HotpotQA and TriviaQA with the model held fixed improves mean ROC-AUC over transferred LOS-Net by 3.76 points.

摘要：

##### **SoK: Grading Open-Text Legal Answers, from Law School Exams to Trustworthy Rubric Judges**
2610.19992v1 by Sebastian Nagl

Law students are examined through open-text answers: case solutions, essays and memos that apply the law to a set of facts. Such answers are expensive to grade, human graders disagree widely on them, and large language models (LLMs) now offer to grade them at scale, both in teaching and in the benchmarks that test legal AI. This paper systematizes how open-text legal answers can be evaluated. We derive eight desiderata from educational measurement and from the law that governs exams, among them validity, reliability, reasons, credit for defensible alternative solutions, robustness to gaming and human oversight. We then compare eight evaluation paradigms against them, from unstructured human grading to LLM judges with instance-specific rubrics and pairwise comparison. Three findings stand out. A rubric written for the task is the strongest lever for agreement between an LLM judge and human graders, but the reported figures answer different questions and rarely come with the human agreement they must be read against. The central validation gap lies where examination law is most demanding: defensible alternative solutions, follow-on errors, and the examiner's correction of model judgments. And rubric-centred grading is the most auditable family, because it makes the standard, its application and the final decision checkable. From this we derive the trustworthy rubric judge: an instance rubric that credits arguments and alternative paths,evidence checked in code, probes, repeated runs, calibration against independent human grades,logging, and an examiner who reads every script. We state what each safeguard rests on, what remains to be tested, and the uses the evidence supports today.

摘要：

##### **Safe-FCL: Continual Risk Awareness and Fine-Grained Safety Aggregation for Federated Continual Learning of LLMs**
2610.19968v1 by Shuaijie Ma, Yongkang Hu, Yu Cheng, Jiuan Zhou, Yi Xie, Yushuo Zhang, Zhaoxia Yin

Federated fine-tuning enables large language models (LLMs) to learn collaboratively from distributed client data, but malicious client updates can compromise global model safety. As federated tasks evolve, changing malicious update patterns challenge the ability of existing defenses to maintain reliable risk detection over time. To address this challenge, we propose Safe-FCL, a framework integrating continual risk awareness with fine-grained safety aggregation for federated continual learning of LLMs. Specifically, the Continual Risk Awareness (CRA) mechanism learns emerging risk patterns across sequential tasks and constrains detector updates using a protective subspace derived from historical features, mitigating forgetting of previously learned malicious patterns while adapting to new risks. To translate risk awareness into aggregation-level safety control, the Fine-Grained Safety Aggregation (FGSA) mechanism combines client-level risk assessments from CRA with module-level deviations from historical update behavior to adaptively regulate aggregation contributions, suppressing potentially malicious updates while minimizing unnecessary restrictions on benign updates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Safe-FCL outperforms current SOTA methods, improving global-model Rule and MD-Judge scores in federated continual learning by 0.41% and 0.64%, respectively, while increasing average risk recognition accuracy by 2.01%.

#### Abstracts
##### **Modeling and Understanding Neural Weights of Humanoid Policies with Diffusion**
2610.20606v1 by Huixuan Cheng, Bike Zhang, Shankar Sastry, Koushil Sreenath

Recent advances in robot learning have driven rapid progress in humanoid control, enabling increasingly agile behaviors in the real world. However, humanoid control networks are typically obtained through conventional training procedures, while comparatively little attention has been given to directly modeling the distribution of their learned weights and generating functional networks in weight space. In this work, we investigate whether high-performing humanoid control networks can be generatively modeled directly through their weights. We adopt a latent diffusion framework that couples a linear autoencoder with a Diffusion Transformer. The autoencoder compresses actor network parameters into a compact latent space, and the Diffusion Transformer models the distribution of these latents. Trained on networks collected from reinforcement learning for the Unitree G1 robot, our approach generates deployable networks that perform various motions in the real world, including walking, dancing, and spin kick. Beyond generation, we further study the learned weight space to better understand the structure captured by these humanoid control networks. We identify a structural principle in the first-layer weights that consistently reflects the robot's kinematic prior. Notably, this kinematic prior emerges under both Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Imitation Learning (IL) training, suggesting that it is not specific to a particular optimization process but may instead reflect common knowledge associated with humanoid embodiment. Together, these results demonstrate the feasibility of generative modeling in humanoid network weight space, while providing initial insights into the information encoded by their learned weights. Additional results are available on our project website: https://humanoid-weight-diffusion.github.io/

摘要：

##### **Hierarchical Residual Policy Learning for Real-World Mobile Manipulation with Sparse Human Guidance**
2610.20607v1 by Zhefei Gong, Hefeifei Jiang, Zehao Wang, Jiachen Li

Mobile Manipulation (MoMa) is a cornerstone of autonomous robotic systems, yet real-world fine-tuning remains a formidable challenge because of high-dimensional action spaces and intricate coupling between heterogeneous modules. In this paper, we propose HiRe-MoMa (Hierarchical Residual Mobile Manipulation), a systematic framework for stable and sample-efficient real-world fine-tuning of complex MoMa systems. At its core, HiRe-MoMa adopts a Hierarchical Residual Architecture that fine-tunes a frozen base policy. A Policy-Agnostic Residual learns additive corrections atop the base policy, while Hierarchical Decoupling splits the residual into mobile-base and manipulator-arm heads with unidirectional conditioning, mitigating gradient interference and aligning corrections with the platform's physical hierarchy. To improve sample efficiency in data-expensive real-world settings, we propose Expert-Guided Value Alignment, which leverages sparse human interventions with under 20% training coverage to reshape the critic's value landscape, effectively guiding autonomous exploration through expert priors. Extensive real-world experiments on the TIAGo++ platform demonstrate that HiRe-MoMa consistently enhances task performance with only around 30 minutes of online interaction, regardless of the base policy architecture. To our knowledge, this is among the first frameworks to enable efficient on-robot fine-tuning for mobile manipulation in the real world, providing a practical paradigm for deploying complex robotic systems in dynamic, human-centric environments. Further details can be found on our project website: https://hire-moma.github.io/

摘要：

##### **Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets**
2610.20587v1 by Shubhendu Trivedi, Jacob Eisenstein, Alekh Agarwal, Ian Gemp, Michael Kaisers, Adam Fisch

An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. An agent's bid is based on its expected value for the prompt, which is the expectation of the reward for its response minus its inference costs. First-price auctions (FPAs), in which the highest bidder pays its bid and receives the prompt, are a natural way to allocate prompts in such a market, since they are simple and transparent to run, and do not require central knowledge of private capabilities and costs. However, FPAs are not truthful: participants may bid less than their true value in the hope of a larger profit, and the prompt may then be allocated to someone other than the bidder who values it the most. This welfare loss is quantified by the $\textit{price-of-anarchy}$ (PoA). In this paper, we argue that agentic markets are likely to possess features that reduce the price-of-anarchy. The first is a $\textit{competitive floor}$, which occurs when multiple bidders usually value the prompt above some baseline share of the optimal welfare (e.g., because the best models are often distilled into cheaper alternatives, which perform similarly). We show that competition among them then limits the welfare loss, which can be driven to zero as the floor rises. The second feature is $\textit{near-independence}$ of values after conditioning on the prompt, which occurs when most of the predictable variation in the expected value is determined by the difficulty of the prompt. As the value distribution approaches conditional independence, we can derive an efficiency guarantee that approaches a factor of $1 - 1/e^2$ of the optimal welfare. Thus, both features give rise to welfare lower bounds that are significantly better than the well-known general case of $1 - 1/e$. We validate these results empirically using auctions with strategic bidders simulated on the EmbedLLM prompt routing dataset.

摘要：

##### **ActiveEvidence: Decision-Focused Evidence Graphs with Verifiable Acquisition Gaps**
2610.20532v1 by Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Peng Zhang, Daren Zha, Jun Xiao

A multimodal agent must decide whether visual evidence is worth acquiring, which candidate or resolution to query, and when further interaction can no longer change its decision. We study this problem under a strict free-metadata/paid-observation boundary, stochastic vector costs, and a frozen execution trace, and treat it as decision-certifiable active evidence acquisition. A Counterfactual Evidence Interaction Graph (CEIG) represents unary evidence value, signed second-order interaction, and directed conditional harm while maintaining explicit score enclosures for unresolved interactions; a decision-weighted enclosure objective tightens the bounds that can change action identity. Gap-directed refinement evaluates only interactions that can displace the incumbent acquisition and stops once an $ε$-decision certificate shows that unresolved terms cannot change the selected action beyond a declared tolerance. The certificate is model-relative: it verifies policy-consistent optimality under the pinned learned score, and semantic quality is measured separately through post-freeze labels. Paired continuation rollouts learn the downstream value of paid previews, an online operational cost filter is separated from independent finite-policy-family trajectory certification, and an acquisition monitor with an independently implemented verifier binds every certificate to the frozen trace. On 640 cases split into 384/128/128 training, validation, and test cases, the controller reaches task score 0.842, grounding F1 0.816, evidence recall 0.873, 1.74 accepted calls, 286.5K visual tokens, and 742ms p95 latency. Dense replay of issued certificates shows 0.992 action agreement and a 0.988 certificate rate while evaluating 19.6 instead of 191.4 expensive interaction terms per decision.

摘要：

##### **ReaDy: Recurrent States as Dynamic Context for Speculative Drafting in Hybrid Linear Attention Large Language Models**
2610.20503v1 by Bohan Sun, Zunhai Su, Xialie Zhuang, Jianjin Li, Ngai Wong, Chuan-Wei Kuo

Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by drafting multiple tokens per step, yet feature-based drafters rely on target hidden features whose contextual emphasis may not align with the needs of subsequent draft positions. Hybrid linear attention (HLA) models maintain compact recurrent states that accumulate historical associations, offering an efficient source of complementary context for speculative drafting. We introduce ReaDy, the first speculative decoding method, to the best of our knowledge, to leverage HLA recurrent states for position-specific context retrieval during drafting. ReaDy equips each draft position with a learned query to retrieve supplementary context directly from the target's committed recurrent states. A lightweight state reader integrates this context into draft representations through residual updates, while low-rank state reads and fused operators keep additional computation and I/O overhead low. Building on DSpark's parallel backbone and sequential prediction head, ReaDy employs a lightweight two-stage adaptation strategy, with reader capacity, state sources, and residual placement examined through ablation studies. Across seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and dialogue, ReaDy achieves an average $3.16\times$ decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding with Qwen3.8-27B on a single NVIDIA B200 GPU. It outperforms DSpark on every benchmark, improving throughput by 7.97% and acceptance length by 8.50% on average. Our code is available at https://github.com/StartLuxLabs/ReaDy.

摘要：

##### **MolLean: A Process-Verifiable Language for Chemical Reasoning**
2610.20459v1 by Jiatong Li, Yatao Bian

Chemical chain-of-thought can contain hallucinated structural and property claims. We thereby introduce MolLean, a process-verifiable language that grounds chemical reasoning in an explicit molecular state. A layered Lean-based kernel checks state transitions and assertions over molecular readouts, from primitive graph edits to compound transformations. Meanwhile, a typed witness protocol separates native structural checks from externally supplied measurements. We further introduce MolLean-MCP, a stateful tool interface that exposes these transitions, checks, and readouts during generation: the model receives feedback to decide its next action, and rejected actions leave the state unchanged. MolLean supports one-pass trace execution, whole-trace refinement, and interactive reasoning through MolLean-MCP. We evaluate these three modes with direct chain-of-thought on 8,800 matched examples across 12 tasks spanning molecular generation, editing, optimization, and reactions. With Qwen3.6-27B, whole-trace refinement outperforms direct chain-of-thought on all 12 tasks. MolLean-MCP complements whole-trace refinement with stepwise verification feedback, reaching better performance in reaction tasks. Together, these results highlight an executable foundation for chemical reasoning, in which explicit chemical commitments can be checked and revised rather than remaining solely in free-form explanations.

摘要：

##### **Doc2Harness: Benchmarking LLMs on Long-Horizon Harness Engineering of Coding Agents**
2610.20344v1 by Yuyao Ge, Shenghua Liu, Peizhong Gao, Chendi Ge, Chenxu Liu, Yiwei Wang, Lingrui Mei, Baolong Bi, Jiayu Yao, Jiafeng Guo, Xueqi Cheng

Agents driven by large language models are increasingly deployed on long-horizon tasks, where they use tools and modify real workspaces. How well they perform depends as much on the harness around the model as on the model itself. Existing benchmarks measure downstream task success under a fixed harness, leaving harness engineering itself unexplored. We propose Doc2Harness, a two-stage benchmark that evaluates long-horizon harness engineering directly. In Stage 1, a build model operating inside a fixed builder harness receives an interface-only contract skeleton and a development document that consolidates behavior across twelve frontier harnesses, and then constructs a complete candidate harness in an isolated container. In Stage 2, the frozen candidate harness runs in containers separate from the construction environment, one per test case, and is driven end to end by a scripted mock model. We score each test case using four observable channels, namely the requests sent to the model, the files on disk, the process exit code, and the command output; positive controls rule out vacuous passes. Across nineteen frontier models, each evaluated with three independent builds on 328 test cases, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest pass rate at 53.05%, while the weakest model reaches 27.44%, a gap of roughly twenty-six percentage points. Even the strongest model fails nearly half of the test cases, and failures cluster around how cross-cutting functionality is split across modules. To our knowledge, Doc2Harness provides the first deterministic benchmark for measuring long-horizon harness engineering.

摘要：

##### **Argument Graph Uncertainty: Quantifying Uncertainty from the Logical Structure of Reasoning Chains**
2610.20328v1 by Simon Baur, Sumire Honda, Wojciech Samek, Jackie Ma

Reliable uncertainty estimation is essential for deploying large language models in high-stakes reasoning tasks, yet most existing approaches rely on expensive sampling strategies or shallow token-level signals that ignore the internal logical structure of model outputs. We introduce Argument Graph Uncertainty, a post-hoc framework for multiple choice question tasks, that estimates model confidence directly from the logical structure of a single chain-of-thought reasoning trace. Our method uses either a lightweight instruction-tuned model or a simple deterministic rule to segment the reasoning chain into propositional units, then applies a NLI model to infer relational edges and construct two complementary argument graphs over these segments. The resulting edge scores are aggregated into a Dirichlet posterior over answer options, whose concentration yields calibrated uncertainty estimates reflecting both local step-to-step coherence and global logical consistency across the reasoning trace. Evaluated on GPQA, MMLU-Pro and TruthfulQA, our method consistently surpasses token-based and consistency-based uncertainty baselines across multiple reasoning models. We further demonstrate through extensive combination analyses that argument graph uncertainty captures an orthogonal signal to existing approaches, making it a complementary component in uncertainty ensembles. Crucially, our framework requires no additional calls to the evaluated model and relies entirely on small, efficient auxiliary models, producing fully interpretable, per-node uncertainty attributions at low computational cost.

摘要：

##### **Taking the PULSE of Medical Reasoning: Step-Level Retrieval Timing for Knowledge-Grounded Inference**
2610.20292v1 by Danyang Zhao, Sendong Zhao, Yanrui Du, Haochun Wang, Ming Ma, Wenhao Li, Jingbo Wang, Jiatong Liu, Bing Qin

Reliable medical reasoning requires LLMs to dynamically access external biomedical evidence to improve factual trustworthiness and reasoning accuracy. However, indiscriminate retrieval can introduce noise and latency, raising a critical retrieval-timing problem: at each reasoning step, the system must decide whether the model's internal knowledge is reliable or external evidence is needed. We propose PUlSE, a lightweight probing method that uses the model's internal representations as intrinsic signals for step-level retrieval control. Our posterior trajectory analysis reveals that erroneous steps exhibit substantially higher inter-layer volatility than correct ones, suggesting unstable internal knowledge. Based on this finding, PUlSE trains a compact probe on inter-layer geometric features to detect factual errors upon step completion, and uses it as an adaptive trigger for retrieval and step regeneration. Across four medical benchmarks, PUlSE-timed retrieval matches exhaustive per-step retrieval in accuracy while reducing retrieval calls by 72%, and can transfer to an unseen legal domain, indicating potential domain-agnostic generality. Overall, our study provides an initial step toward shifting retrieval-trigger design from surface-level confidence estimates to internal activation dynamics for adaptive access.

摘要：

##### **Repairing Speech Access After Text Updates with Frozen Affine Fields**
2610.20253v1 by Ming-Hao Hsu, Yuxiang Wang, Yingda Shen, Zhizheng Wu

Speech-capable language models inherit most of their knowledge from text-rich language backbones, and continued model maintenance is likewise dominated by text updates. Knowledge acquired through a text update can nevertheless remain difficult to access when the same query is spoken. We formulate this mismatch as post-update speech access repair, where the knowledge-bearing checkpoint stays frozen and a detachable operator repairs speech-only access in one model pass. Low-rank ReFT interventions also transfer positively, whereas the matched SALSA variant falls 0.0707 below its paired baseline. These results show that text acquisition and speech access can diverge after an update and should be verified and repaired separately.

##### **Retrieval Over Training: Similarity search-based Model Selection for Time Series Anomaly Detection**

Anomaly detection is a fundamental task for time-series analytics, with important implications for the downstream performance of many applications. Despite the large number of anomaly detection methods proposed in the literature, recent benchmark studies have shown that no single detector performs best across highly heterogeneous time series. Therefore, a practical and scalable solution is to develop a model-selection method that, for a given time series, selects the anomaly detector most likely to perform well. Nevertheless, the model selection approaches proposed in the literature suffer from a significant drop in accuracy when applied in Out-of-Distribution (OOD) settings. In this paper, we tackle the aforementioned limitation and propose RAMSAD, a retrieval-based framework for model selection in time-series anomaly detection. Given a new time series, our method queries a knowledge base of previously observed instances, retrieves the top-k most similar series, and transfers detector recommendations from their performance profiles. The framework can operate with standard similarity measures as well as embedding-based representations. Our results show that RAMSAD outperforms existing baselines in In-Distribution (ID) settings and is the most robust selector in OOD, where, when selecting a small subset of detectors, it matches or exceeds ensembles that run the entire detector pool, at a fraction of their cost. ##### **Drivers of LLM Consensus Formation Under Competing Incentives: A Simulation Study**

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent environments that produce unanimous agreement, both in intended interactions that need coordination and in unintended emergent interactions. We introduce an evaluation, based on the Network Consensus game, to assess the mechanisms driving this process. Here, a network of agents must agree on one of two colours to receive a payoff, but agents are offered different payoffs for the agreed colour.   We find that open-source LLMs reach consensus less often than humans in comparable experiments and only if messages are communicated across the global network. This is achieved through lower stubbornness and higher majority-following tendencies, and by providing more information about its neighbours' states in messages. 2610.19557v1 by Aditya Radhakrishnan, Maxwell A. Xu, Wanting Mao, Santosh Kumar, Harish Haresamudram, James M. Rehg

Contrastive learning for time series depends on deciding which pairs of inputs should be treated as similar and dissimilar. We introduce TEX-MEX, to our knowledge the first approach to unify the two into a single relationship. Given a random batch of windows drawn across the dataset, TEX-MEX uses the encoder to identify which windows are structurally similar or dissimilar to each other. Risk estimation supplied efficiency, matching that safety with 31% fewer validators at somewhat lower yield, although better targeting than random validation was not shown. Lineage evidence helped on one workflow. The language model added no measurable benefit: whether invalid results escaped depended on the harness, not the model. This proof of concept points to a practical path: placing agents inside explicit release-control structure can keep released scientific results trustworthy, and it gives more capable agents a measurable baseline to improve on.

##### **Dimension-Free Scaling Laws for Size Generalization: An Exponential Separation**
2610.19279v1 by Behrooz Tahmasebi, Melanie Weber

Modern machine learning models increasingly operate on variable-size structured data, such as sets and graphs. A central requirement is \emph{size generalization}: the ability of a model trained on inputs of one size to generalize to inputs of a different, potentially much larger, size. Classical statistical learning theory, however, primarily considers fixed input domains, and its guarantees often deteriorate with input dimension. Although extensive work has established when models on sets and graphs can be transferred across input sizes, such results do not characterize the statistical cost of doing so: the sample complexity of size generalization, and its relationship to classical in-domain learning, remain largely unknown. We develop a statistical learning theory of size generalization and establish a sharp exponential separation in sample complexity. Letting $n$ denote the number of training samples, we show that polynomial source decay is insufficient for efficient size generalization: while it yields error $n^{-Θ(1)}$ and hence polynomial sample complexity for in-domain learning, size-generalization error decays only as $(\log n)^{-Θ(1)}$, yielding an exponential separation. Thus, polynomial in-domain sample complexity can become exponential across sizes. We then identify an exponential source condition under which size generalization achieves error $n^{-Θ(1)}$, recovering polynomial sample complexity. These guarantees are independent of input dimension and depend only on the number of training samples, yielding dimension-free scaling laws. We also provide matching minimax lower bounds, establishing optimality. To our knowledge, this work provides the first dimension-free minimax theory of size generalization and a rigorous characterization of when, and at what statistical cost, learning can transfer across input sizes.

Federated fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) allows multiple clients to collaboratively adapt models without sharing raw data, offering strong privacy benefits. However, this setting is highly vulnerable to backdoor attacks, in which malicious clients manipulate their updates to induce targeted behaviors while maintaining normal performance on benign inputs.
  In this work, we make a key observation specific to federated LLM fine-tuning: malicious clients tend to exhibit strong consistency in their model updates, whereas benign clients produce more diverse and dispersed updates. This structural difference is not well captured by existing defense methods, which typically rely on direct anomaly detection in high-dimensional parameter space and therefore struggle in the presence of entangled LLM updates.
  Motivated by this observation, we propose FedGraph, a graph-based aggregation framework that explicitly models relationships among client updates. By representing clients as nodes in a dynamically constructed graph and extracting structural features that characterize their relative positions and interactions, FedGraph derives compact client representations that effectively separate malicious and benign behaviors. These representations are then used to identify and suppress malicious contributions during aggregation.
  Extensive experiments demonstrate that FedGraph significantly improves backdoor defense. It consistently reduces attack success rates to below 10% while achieving high detection accuracy (95.5% on average) and a low false positive rate (2.33%), substantially outperforming existing federated learning defenses.

##### **Agaram 2.5B: The First Tamil-First Language Model Trained from Scratch with Tool Calling. A Playbook for Under-Represented Languages**

We trained Agaram 2.5B, a 2.5-billion-parameter Tamil-English language model, from scratch on about 50 billion tokens (58% Tamil, the rest English, math, code and parallel text) on one node of eight H100 GPUs in about six days, with a new tokenizer that, unlike our first model's, keeps line breaks. Supervised fine-tuning on 134,428 conversations, whose replies an open-weight teacher model wrote, and three short targeted rounds turned it into a chat model that answers in Tamil script, romanized Tamil (Tanglish) and English and can call tools: on 25 tool prompts whose schemas appear in training it picks the right tool 23 times and gives exactly the expected arguments 13 times. The released model translates better than Sarvam-1, a 2B Indic base model, in all six FLORES and IN22 directions (FLORES chrF 54.3 into Tamil and 55.2 into English, against 49.2 and 44.2), and nearly doubles its base model on native-Tamil IndicIFEval (19.3% from 10.0%). It is weak on knowledge: reading-comprehension and knowledge benchmarks sit near chance, and on 50 held-out everyday questions written by one native speaker, one LLM judge rated 46% of its replies usable (22-24 of 50 over three passes; 95% interval for 23 of 50, 33-60%), with nearly every failure a wrong fact. Our two further SFT experiments changed answer format but not the knowledge measures. We also describe our first model, Agaram 1.5B, whose failures shaped the choices here; how the judge's scores varied between passes on identical replies; and, item by item, what the targeted rounds trained against and where frontier-model text entered the data. The project ran on compute credits and our own funds, about USD 13,300 across both models; the weights are released (licence to be announced after legal review), with GGUF files for llama.cpp, at https://huggingface.co/murai-labs/agaram-2.5b-instruct; code at https://github.com/Murai-Labs/Agaram2.5b

##### **Delegating Earth science model calibration to knowledge-guided LLM agents**
2610.18378v1 by Zhanliang Zhu, Ziwei Li, Liujun Zhu, Yuchen Liu, Junliang Jin, Jianyun Zhang

Applying process-based Earth science models to new sites and questions often requires expert calibration decisions. Reliable delegation to language-model agents must preserve scientific competence and reproducibility. Here we develop a general calibration harness (GCH) for end-to-end delegation from a calibration request to assessed results. Constrained by a knowledge infrastructure (KI) and a validation convention, agents define parameter domains, observation mappings and objectives, and generate model-specific code. GCH checks the formulations against the same declarations and freezes them before numerical search and assessment, which require no further agent judgement. Agent-authored workflows executed across six model families, with assessment identifying unmet criteria. In snow-model tests, five of five knowledge-guided specifications executed on the first attempt, compared with none of five given only the model name, achieving validation Nash-Sutcliffe efficiency (NSE) of 0.81-0.94 against 0.57 for defaults. Under a common objective, independent GR4J formulations yielded scores within 0.001 NSE; re-evaluating eleven saved parameter vectors reproduced their metrics. ##### **Pin It on Anyone: Certified Targeted Displacement of Multi-Bit LLM Watermarks Without the Key**
2610.18359v1 by Paweł Batorski, Przemysław Spurek, Paul Swoboda

Multi-bit watermarking embeds a message, typically an account identifier, into LLM output so that harmful text can be traced to its origin. We show an attack technique that allows to alter the watermarking message. This allows to, for example, exchange the embedded account ID to an arbitrary different one. The attacker mainly needs access to a few accounts for generating LLM output and model logits, but crucially not the secret key used to watermark. The attacker then rewrites the text, keeping its meaning, so that the watermarking message decoder recovers the watermarking message embedded by the attacker. We are, to our knowledge, the first to propose such an attack, which we call a targeted displacement. Theoretically, we present upper and lower bounds on the probability of our attack succeeding. This bound depends on the number of accounts the attacker has access to, the length of the message and some characteristics of the given text. Our attack can succeed whenever we can bound the ambiguity of the generated text well enough. Empirically, we test our attack technique against five watermarking methods and four LLMs. Attack success tracks our predicted bounds, allowing us to alter watermarking messages in almost all cases with high accuracy, given enough accounts. Our code can be found at https://github.com/Batorskq/PinIt.

##### **ABot-ER: Unified Embodied Reasoning through Agentic Data-Model Co-Evolution**
2610.18355v2 by Qiuyu Ding, Heng-Da Xu, Changda Xia, Jiajun Shen, Zihao Guan, Zhengchen Yuan, Jianfang Zhang, Di Yang, Jiayi Tian, Yuting Xu, Fei Wang, Shiao Liu, Wei Zhang, Xiaolong Wu, Wenbin Tang, Zedong Chu, Mu Xu

We introduce ABot Embodied Reasoning (ABot-ER), a unified embodied reasoning foundation model that breaks the tension between breadth and depth in embodied reasoning. Embodied intelligence demands the joint mastery of perception, planning, and execution, yet reconciling generality with specialization across this spectrum remains an open challenge. Within a single unified model, ABot-ER commands specialist-level depth across all three competencies, spanning spatial, scene, and temporal reasoning in perception; long-horizon action organization in planning; and point grounding and affordance in execution. The Agentic Data-Model Self-Evolution Engine continuously adapts data strategies to the model's evolving capability state. The Task-Conditioned Adaptive Training Strategy uses task tokens to activate lightweight low-rank residuals over a shared backbone, separating updates from heterogeneous output formats while retaining shared embodied knowledge. Across a systematic evaluation on twelve benchmarks covering three core competencies and five capability dimensions, ABot-ER achieves state-of-the-art overall performance across perception, planning, and execution within a unified parametric framework, demonstrating that breadth and depth can coexist in unified embodied reasoning.

##### **Complex materials engineering via Provenance Action Graphs: potential, structure and demonstration**
2610.18314v1 by Yevgeny Rakita, Paul Todd, James R. Neilson, Simon J. L. Billinge

This work discusses and tests the potential of reaction graphs for material synthesis, in which events consist of reaction steps (actions) and their outputs (observations). The importance comes from the requirement to synthesize technologically useful materials, especially those that are kinetically stabilized. Often, it is a science that follows years of trial and error, culminating in (published) successful recipes, but is truly driven by far more (unpublished) failures. The entry into the era of AI provides an opportunity to use learning algorithms to encode rules from multi-dimensional synthesis pathways, regardless of their technological usefulness, and to decode both forward prediction and inverse learning modes. The main incentive is to accelerate material discovery by generating a global knowledge base that can be shared among data agents and contributed to by them. We will focus on several scenarios in the common synthesis process during solid-state synthesis, primarily because these reactions are kinetic in nature and require closer monitoring and control of the reaction history.

##### **LegalRewardBench: Benchmarking Reward Models for Legal Preference Evaluation**
2610.18299v1 by Hui Huang, Jie Zhou, Hongli Zhou, Qundong Shi, Shuyuan Zheng, Yini Su, Shaochun Wang, Biyuan Lin, Muyun Yang, Tiejun Zhao

Reward models are central to preference alignment for legal tasks, yet existing reward model benchmarks are still primarily centered on general-domain preferences. In this work, we introduce LegalRewardBench, the first human-verified benchmark for evaluating reward models in the legal domain. The benchmark organizes legal preference evaluation into five capability levels: knowledge, understanding, analysis, decision, and writing, and preference examples are constructed through rubric-guided automatic judging combined with human verification. We evaluate three families of judges, covering both general and fine-tuned models, and generative and discriminative architectures. Experimental results show that strong general-purpose generative models are currently the most robust legal reward models, while existing fine-tuned reward models transfer less reliably to legal preference evaluation. ##### **Output Discipline Sets the Floor: A Falsification-Guided, Model-Agnostic Agent Protocol for Auditable Hypothesis-Driven Decision Making**
2610.18298v1 by Jing Liu, Yongxing Qi, Ping Yao, Ting Wu

What binds large language models on auditable, hypothesis-driven decision tasks, where every candidate can be checked against the evidence? We test this with a model-agnostic agent framework formalizing decision making as a five-stage closed loop, enforced by a candidate x evidence matrix falsification protocol: every candidate is judged against every evidence item, at least half of the candidates must be falsified before a conclusion is licensed, and survivors are emitted in a machine-verifiable single-line format. Across five base models, the effect is sign-conditional on zero-shot output discipline: it rescues models whose discipline fails (DeepSeek-V4-Flash: +0.267/+0.271, entirely termination rescue), reduces a high-variance model's run-to-run standard deviation from 0.218 to 0.007 (Kimi K3), and significantly costs already-disciplined models (-0.087 to -0.120 on budget-matched Qwen3-4B/32B). Ablations attribute the cost to the candidate-bounded format layer and show the effect is independent of injected knowledge content (protocol-knowledge decoupling). The protocol also transfers to misinformation-robust mathematical reasoning, detecting injected false premises at 89.8% recall with a 6.8% false-positive rate (MCC 0.54; a flag-everything baseline scores 0) and restoring accuracy to the no-misinformation ceiling. Where zero-shot output discipline is weak, the reasoning protocol sets the floor; base-model capability always sets the ceiling.

##### **Are We Testing Too Much? A Survey of Benchmark Compression for Efficient Evaluation of Large Language Models**
2610.18248v1 by Guoming Ling, Yupei Lin, Zhongzhan Huang, Junxin Li, Junjie Liu, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, Liang Lin

Benchmarks are essential for evaluating and improving large language models (LLMs). Yet many widely used benchmarks are redundant, making evaluation unnecessarily expensive. Benchmark compression lowers this cost by estimating full-benchmark scores and rankings from a partial evaluation. Research in this area has grown rapidly since 2024, but to our knowledge no survey has systematically organized its methods and findings. We review 119 papers to explain how methods choose what to evaluate, recover full-benchmark results, and verify their reliability. From this analysis, we develop a taxonomy covering compression within individual benchmarks, across benchmark suites, and during adaptive evaluation, together with work on redundancy and reliability. We compare their information needs, total costs, reliability checks, and limitations. Methods reduce evaluation costs by selecting representative subsets, using cheaper or reusable information, and testing adaptively, but risk poor coverage, inaccurate estimates, and sequential delays. The resulting taxonomy and practical guide help researchers choose, validate, and deploy methods for more efficient and reliable LLM evaluation.

##### **QORH: Learning Query-Specific Ordered Rule Hypergraphs for Inductive Knowledge Graph Link Prediction**
2610.18198v1 by Naixing Xu

Knowledge graph link prediction involving unseen entities and relations requires learning transferable reasoning patterns from observed graph structure. Existing methods based on relation graphs primarily capture pairwise dependencies, limiting their ability to preserve the compositional semantics, relation ordering, and role information of complete path rules. To address this limitation, we propose QORH (Query-specific Ordered Rule Hypergraph), which jointly encodes rule heads and ordered rule bodies as hyperedges conditioned on the query relation, explicitly modeling joint dependencies among multiple relations. QORH performs learnable rule selection using statistical evidence, such as rule confidence and coverage, and enhances relation representations through role-aware hypergraph message passing to guide entity-level reasoning. The model's shared parameters are independent of specific entity or relation identifiers, enabling inductive prediction for unseen entities and relations. Extensive experiments on entity-inductive and fully inductive benchmarks demonstrate that QORH significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art baselines included in our evaluation, validating the effectiveness of rule hypergraph modeling for inductive link prediction.

##### **Fresh at Decision, Stale at Action: Routing New Information to LLM Decisions Already Made**
2610.18126v1 by Ayoub Merimi, Souhail Bakkali, Abdeljalil Sakat

Freshness shapes an LLM decision at the moment it is made; the action it commits to is latent, carried out later, in a changing world. We therefore introduce a timestamp theory of decision validity and a decision ledger that realises it. Six timestamps define a decision; a freshness ceiling bounds what up-to-date evidence guarantees at action time, placing the remaining risk in information published afterwards. Accuracy then factorises into two capabilities: routing, which delivers that information to affected decisions before their revision deadlines, and temporal judgment, which reads it for the time of action. The ledger is persistent state holding each decision's times, evidence and background knowledge, indexed by watch conditions written at decision time. Controlled decision streams with exact labels confirm four predictions across five LLMs, from 8B to frontier scale: freshness alone yields exactly the accuracy the ceiling predicts; models act on decisive news once it arrives, so accuracy follows routing; the ledger's index sets what routing reaches, and watch conditions reach indirect dependencies at low exposure; and the remaining errors concern when a change takes effect. Freshness makes a decision right when it is made; routing and temporal judgment keep it right when it is carried out.

##### **Certifying Almost-Sure Reachability in Unknown MDPs**
2610.18119v1 by S Akshay, Suguman Bansal, Gaurav Mahla

{\em Specification-Guided Reinforcement Learning (RL)} has largely concerned itself with \emph{learning} an optimal policy against a formal specification in an unknown {\em Markov Decision Process (MDP)}. We take up the dual question. Given a policy already in hand, can one \emph{verify} that it is correct against a formal specification in an unknown MDP, using sampling access alone? Such a question arises naturally in safety-critical systems. We investigate it for the {\em almost-sure reachability property}, whose correctness is witnessed by a {\em ranking supermartingale (RSM)} certificate. Our first finding is negative. In the absence of any further information about the MDP, one can neither learn such a certificate nor verify a candidate one with probabilistic guarantees. On the positive side, we show that, under a mild restriction, a known lower bound $p_{\min}$ on nonzero transition probabilities, a correct certificate \emph{can} be learned with PAC-style guarantees. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study of learning and verifying certificates for almost-sure reachability in unknown MDPs from sampling access alone. Finally, our experiments show that the $p_{\min}$-based sufficient condition, while sound, is conservative: the learned certificate often remains valid on the true MDP even when the condition fails.

##### **When Missingness Follows the Graph: Beyond Feature Propagation for Missing Features**
2610.18113v1 by Enrico Chialà, Francesco Ferrini, Antonio Longa, Bruno Lepri, Filippo Maria Bianchi, Manfred Jaeger, Veronica Lachi

Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) commonly assume that node features are fully observed, despite missing features being pervasive in real-world applications. By leveraging the dependence between node features and graph structure, existing graph-specific methods handle missingness by propagating observed feature values along graph edges. However, these methods have mainly been evaluated in overly optimistic settings, where feature entries are removed independently and uniformly across the graph. This overlooks realistic scenarios in which missingness propagates among connected nodes, as can occur during localized sensing failures in sensor networks. We formalize this complementary notion as graph-structured mask dependence, where the joint distribution of missingness indicators is shaped by the graph, and introduce a controlled mechanism that instantiates this setting. We study propagation-based methods theoretically and empirically in this setting, showing that their effectiveness changes substantially compared with the uniformly random regime. Motivated by this limitation, we propose PEMix, designed to remain effective under challenging graph-structured missingness. Rather than propagating observed values, PEMix uses global structural representations to condition a probabilistic model of incomplete node features before message passing. Experiments under controlled graph-structured missingness and naturally occurring missingness show that PEMix remains robust across complex missingness regimes, with particularly strong performance as missingness becomes severe.

##### **AReaL-RSI: Adaptive Scaling and Coordination of Recursive Self-Improvement**

Scaling recursive self-improvement requires additional computation to produce useful research progress in reaching persistent objectives. Intuitively, more researcher agents can explore more hypotheses, but early evaluation scores might misrepresent their promise, shared findings could lose their applicability conditions, and useful procedures could remain confined to completed investigations. In this paper, we present AReaL-RSI, a framework for organizing a persistent research team of agents without updating backbone LLM weights. We formulate the scaling problem in terms of evaluated outcomes and whole-system resource expenditure. Concretely, a leader agent selects task-appropriate research orientations and guides ongoing investigations; conditional memory sharing distinguishes unresolved problems from supported findings and enables recipient adaptation. During recursive evolution, after each campaign, evidence-based review revises researcher orientation, knowledge, and skills for later campaigns. Experiments compare AReaL-RSI with Codex-Goal on downstream harness optimization, GPU kernel optimization, and AI research tasks using two backbones. Under the reported resource allocations, AReaL-RSI improves Terminal Bench performance by 2.2 percentage points for each backbone and increases mean KernelBench speedup by 32.2-36.8%; further scalability comparisons favor the design of AReaL-RSI over native scaling: combining experience sharing with specialized researcher roles yields 1.9-27.5% lower kernel latency than independent exploration and 1.3-33.9% lower latency than sharing alone across all seven comparable task-backbone pairs.

##### **Uncertainty Evaluation: Bridging Machine Learning and Measurement Science**
2610.18066v1 by Dario Petri, Luca Mari

Reliable uncertainty evaluation is essential for assessing the trustworthiness of machine learning (ML) predictions, particularly in safety-critical applications. This paper explores conceptual analogies between uncertainty evaluation in ML and measurement science, drawing on the evolution of metrology from error-based approaches to the framework established by the Guide to the Expression of Uncertainty in Measurement (GUM). Particular attention is devoted to the distinction between process-level and result-level quality and to the limitations of the conventional classification of ML uncertainty into aleatoric and epistemic components. It is argued that this dichotomy, similarly to the traditional distinction between random and systematic measurement errors, is inherently model-dependent and provides an insufficient basis for a unified uncertainty evaluation framework. Further analogies between machine learning and measurement field of knowledge are identified concerning distribution shifts, anomalous operating conditions, and the validity of uncertainty evaluation. Building on the proposed considerations, the paper advocates a metrology-inspired approach based on the systematic identification, quantification, and propagation of uncertainty contributions. By extending the methodology already established in measurement science to ML, this approach could offer a potentially rigorous, consistent, and transferable framework for evaluating the trustworthiness of individual predictions.

2610.17928v1 by Taeyun Roh, Suhyeong Park, Gijin Kim, Wonjune Jang, Jaewoo Kang

Recent advances in vision-language models (VLMs) have substantially improved the capabilities of GUI agents, while memory augmentation has emerged as a promising approach for reusing knowledge from past interactions. Motivated by the finding, we introduce \textbf{SwitchMem}, a routing framework that dynamically selects between textual and visual memory representations at each step. ##### **LinMorph: Learning to Morph Weights for Efficient Video DiT Linearization**
2610.17914v1 by Lingyi Long, Congyue Zhao, Xiaoou Li, Yanmin Qian, Tao Huang

Post-hoc linearization is an attractive way to accelerate pretrained Video Diffusion Transformers (Video DiTs), yet existing methods largely treat the quality loss induced by attention replacement as a recovery problem. Random initialization discards pretrained knowledge, whereas manual weight reuse transfers it through rigid and incomplete source--target correspondences. We introduce \textbf{LinMorph}, which recasts Video DiT linearization as a learnable softmax-to-linear weight transformation problem. Instead of copying predefined counterparts, LinMorph learns a cross-parameter morphism that lets each target Gated DeltaNet (GDN) parameter draw jointly from pretrained query, key, value, and output projections, including for parameters with no direct softmax counterpart. A single morphism is shared across layers and learned through local teacher alignment, while producing layer-specific initializations from their respective pretrained weights. The resulting weights are then materialized as independent GDN modules for progressive distillation and joint recovery.Since the morphism operates in weight space rather than token space, it can be learned on short, low-resolution clips and directly reused at larger video scales. Across pretrained Video DiTs, LinMorph enables markedly faster recovery and better final generation quality than random initialization and heuristic weight reuse. ##### **Cross-Lingual Transfer of Evil Vectors Is Directionally Asymmetric**
2610.17880v1 by Juann Kim, Minku Kang

Persona vectors are linear directions in the residual-stream activations of LLMs that encode behavioral traits and enable fine-grained control over model behavior. Among them, the evil persona vector is particularly relevant to understanding harmful behavior, jailbreak vulnerabilities, and the unlearning of harmful knowledge. Yet whether this vector reflects a genuinely cross-lingual concept, rather than an artifact of its extraction language, remains untested. We conduct a systematic evil-vector transfer study across two high-resource languages (English, Spanish) and two low-resource languages (Swahili, Telugu) on four open-source instruction-tuned LLMs, and find that transfer is directionally asymmetric. Evil vectors are harder to obtain reliably from low-resource languages, and the resulting vectors are largely ineffective at steering, even within their own language. In contrast, vectors extracted from high-resource languages transfer effectively to low-resource inputs while largely preserving general knowledge, as measured by MMLU. Geometric analysis further supports this pattern: evil vectors from the two high-resource languages are closely aligned, whereas low-resource vectors align only weakly with this shared direction. These results suggest that a shared cross-lingual evil direction exists and, once reliably extracted from a high-resource language, can be reused to steer behavior in low-resource languages without additional per-language data collection.
